## Document 317: EcoCharts _ Business creations and bankruptcies - Partial update with monthly data as of September 2026 (2026-11-13)

Source PDF: all report pdf/EcoCharts _ Business creations and bankruptcies - Partial update with monthly data as of September 2026 (2026-11-13).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13/11/2026 1
November 2026
BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IN FRANCE: DOUBLE-DIGIT GROWTH IN BUSINESS CREATIONS,
LOWER INCREASE IN BANKRUPTCIES, AND STABLE NON-PERFORMING LOANS – PARTIAL UPDATE WITH
MONTHLY DATA AS OF SEPTEMBER 2026
ECO CHARTS | BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IS A NEW PUBLICATION AIMED AT PROVIDING AN OVERVIEW OF THE DYNAMICS
OF BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IN FRANCE, AS WELL AS THE RISKS ASSOCIATED WITH THE LATTER. WE WILL PUBLISH

REGULAR UPDATES TO THIS PUBLICATION.

### Page 2

Double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans
2ECOCHARTSECONOMIC STUDIES
• Business creations rose by 16% y/y as of Q3 2026, marking an acceleration compared with the growth seen in 2025 (5.3% y/y).
• This increase was driven by sectors associated with new information and communication technologies, where the trend is gaining
momentum.
• Meanwhile, business bankruptcies rose by 1.7% y/y as of Q3 2026, according to the data from the Banque de France (vs. 3.6% y/y in

2025). After a more pronounced rise in the first quarter (8% y/y), this increase has since slowed. This moderation could continue, as,
according to Altares, payment delays have decreased to an average of 12.3 days in the second quarter of 2026 (14.1 days a year
earlier), compared with the European average of 13.1 days.
• The rise in business bankruptcies is most pronounced in the services sector, which has simultaneously seen an increase in business

creations. This trend is clear in the retail sector, with a 26% y/y increase in business creations as of Q3 2026, while business
bankruptcies are up 3% y/y.
• In construction and manufacturing, business bankruptcies have been declining since Q2 2026.
• The level of the average ratio of non-performing loans is moderately high and has remained stable for the past year (4.0% of total
loans and advances in Q2 2026). More cyclical than in other sectors, the ratio of non-performing loans in construction reached its

highest level since 2019, at 9.5%.
• The share of underperforming loans, but not yet non-performing (in Stage 2, according to IFRS 9 terminology), which provides an
indication of the future trend in non-performing loans, is stable, standing at 15.4% q/q, and remains below its all-time high (16.1%).
• Despite a significant rise since 2021, the economic weight of bankruptcies — which allows them to be weighted by a proxy for
company size — remains in September, on average, below its Q1 2025 peak (0.54 compared with 0.61, respectively). The economic

weight of bankrupted small businesses decreased but still stands at a high level (2.03).
For more information, please refer to our publication: Chart of the week | French corporates: peaks in creations put peaks in bankruptcies into
perspective. July 22, 2026 – Economic Research – BNP Paribas

Business creations Insee Monthly
Survey of business creations (all types, companies, sole traders, micro-entrepreneurs). Monthly data from
INSEE aggregated over a quarter to minimise the risk of calendar effects

## Document 355: Is the economy ‘plucked’_ (2026-11-27)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Is the economy ‘plucked’_ (2026-11-27).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-27
Pages: 31
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

A New Keynesian ‘plucking’ model of unemployment and inflation
PRELIMINARY AND INCOMPLETE
Anthony Brassil* and Callum Ryan*
Research Discussion Paper
20XX-YY
December 2025
*Economic Research Department
Reserve Bank of Australia
The views expressed in this paper are those of the authors and should not be attributed to the
Reserve Bank of Australia. Any errors are the sole responsibility of the authors.
Author: ryanca at domain rba.gov.au
External Communications: rbainfo@rba.gov.au

https://doi.org/10.47688/rdp20XX-YY

### Page 2

Abstract
We construct a New Keynesian model with search-and-matching frictions in the labour market that
can generate the nonlinear ‘plucking’ dynamics of unemployment in the data: unemployment tends to
spike upwards and then slowly decline. The model features endogenous layoffs, a ‘slippery’ job ladder,
downward nominal wage rigidity, and convex recruiting costs. It generates both the ‘deepness’ and
‘steepness’ asymmetries in unemployment dynamics, and broadly matches the response of labour-

market flows in a downturn. The model endogeneously generates a Phillips curve relationship that
is strongly nonlinear and state-dependent, and a short-run non-accelerating rate of unemployment
(NAIRU) that fluctuates asymmetrically over the business cycle. We also show that cyclical properties
(e.g. shock volatility, nominal rigidities, policy regime) can have a significant effect on the long-run
NAIRU via agents’ precautionary response to future uncertainty.

JEL Classification Numbers:
Keywords:

### Page 4

Figure 1: Unemployment rate in Australia
*Long-run NAIRU estimate from Ballantyne and Cusbert (2025).
1. Introduction
The unemployment rate in the data has strongly nonlinear dynamic (Figure 1). It spikes sharply in
occasional downturns, then declines slowly but persistently in recoveries.1 It is right-skewed in both
levels (i.e. asymmetric ‘deepness’) and in changes (i.e. asymmetric ‘steepness’) (Sichel 1993). Gross
flows between unemployment and employment display similar nonlinearities. Standard search-and-

matching models of the labour market struggle to generate these nonlinearities in unemployment
and labour-market flows (Ferraro 2023).
If this asymmetric pattern stems from frictions within the labour market, what implication do those
frictions have for the relationship between inflation and unemployment? Research using nonlinear
structural models to explore the asymmetric dynamics in the labour market tend to abstract from price
frictions and inflation dynamics (e.g. Dupraz, Nakamura and Steinsson 2025; Ferraro 2018; Petrosky-

Nadeau, Zhang and Kuehn 2018), while in the New Keynesian literature, the usual approach of
linearising equilibrium conditions excludes these nonlinear dynamics by assumption. The 2020s
inflation surge has reignited research into the nonlinearity of the Phillips curve, and the extent to which
this nonlinearity stems from frictions within the labour market (e.g. Benigno and Eggertsson 2023).
But this literature tends not to model the labour market in as much detail.

In this paper, we explore the extent to which a detailed search-and-matching model of the labour
market with pricing frictions can match the nonlinear dynamics in the labour market. We then use
the model to examine the implications of these nonlinearities for the Phillips curve relationship and
the non-accelerating rate of unemployment (NAIRU).
1 Friedman (1964) uses the ‘plucking’ description for the asymmetric way in which output seems to be occasionally ‘plucked’

below trend, before recovering back to trend.

### Page 5

2
We start with a general-equilibrium search-and-matching model similar to Bloesch, Lee and
Weber (2024), in which firms post wages and vacancies and set prices (subject to nominal rigidities
and recruiting costs), and workers search (while unemployed and on the job) and decide whether to
accept and quit jobs. Policy steers aggregate demand to balance inflation and unemployment targets.
We extend this model to include endogenous layoffs, downward nominal wage rigidity, and a ‘slippery’

job ladder (drawing on Dupraz et al (2025)).2 We find that these features - together with convex
recruiting costs - are collectively key to achieving both the asymmetric spikes in unemployment when
shocks occur and the slow speed in the subsequent recovery. The model is sufficiently detailed to
broadly match the dynamics of unemployment and gross flows, but tractable enough that we can
obtain the global nonlinear solution with rational expectations and aggregate uncertainty. This is

important because agents’ precautionary response to future uncertainty is a significant determinant
of outcomes, especially the long-run NAIRU.
We show that the model can generate substantial asymmetry in both ‘deepness’ and ‘steepness’
(comparable to or greater than in the data). The response of unemployment, job-finding, and
separations after a stylised negative demand shock broadly matches the response of these variables in
downturns over the past 50 years. Aggregate demand shocks have an especially asymmetric effect,

because the effect on inflation amplifies the effect of the downward nominal wage rigidity. When
supply shocks occur, the inflation response somewhat eases the downward nominal wage constraint.
The model endogenously generates a Phillips curve relationship that is strongly nonlinear and state-
dependent. Conditional on some initial state of the economy, the Phillips curve is much steeper for
low rates of unemployment. When the initial unemployment rate is higher, the Phillips curve shifts to

the right, and when the initial level of real wages is higher, it shifts upwards.
We can also use the model to simulate a ‘short-run NAIRU’, which is the unemployment rate consistent
with eliminating inflationary pressure from the labour market at some cyclical horizon (e.g. 1-2 years).
This short-run NAIRU varies asymmetrically with the state of the economy. It tends to rise when
unemployment is high, but also when unemployment has fallen rapidly (because real wages increase).

Finally, we can use the ‘stochastic steady state’ of the model to explore the determinants of the long-
run NAIRU, i.e. the unemployment rate consistent with stable inflation in the absence of shocks (but
where agents still expect that shocks may occur in the future). We find that when firms account for
the interaction between aggregate risk and asymmetric wage adjustment costs in their wage-setting
decisions, it generates a trade-off between unemployment variance and steady-state unemployment.

For example, if policy places greater weight on reducing the gap between unemployment and the
long-run NAIRU (instead of the gap between inflation and target), it tends to increase the long-run
NAIRU. This is because by mitigating fluctuations in labour demand, policy reduces the risk of hitting
downward nominal wage rigidities. Firms are then more willing to set higher real wages in steady
state, which reduces the optimal amount of employment in steady state.

The remainder of this introduction discusses the literature. Section 2 outlines the model. Section 3
evaluates the extent to which it can match the nonlinear dynamics in labour-market data. Section 4
considers the implications for the Phillips curve and NAIRU.
2 By ‘slippery’ job ladder, we mean that workers recently hired out of unemployed are much more likely to be laid off.

### Page 6

3
Literature review. The cyclical asymmetries in unemployment are established empirically by e.g.
Sichel (1993), McKay and Reis (2008), and Dupraz et al (2025). Ferraro (2023) analyses the ability of
the baseline Diamond-Mortensen-Pissarides (DMP) model to produce these asymmetries, and finds
that it cannot generate the ‘steepness’ asymmetry (i.e. right-skewness in unemployment changes).
Petrosky-Nadeau et al (2018) finds that the DMP model can generate the ‘deepness’ asymmetry given

large enough shocks.
Dupraz et al (2025) and Ferraro (2018) propose search-and-matching models that can generate
sharp spikes and slow declines in unemployment. We take several features from Dupraz et al (2025),
including downward nominal wage rigidity and a ‘slippery’ job ladder. Pizzinelli, Theodoridis and
Zanetti (2020) explore the state-dependence of unemployment fluctuations in a model with
endogenous layoffs and on-the-job search.3 Adjemian, Karamé and Langot (2021) estimate a

nonlinear model with endogenous separations, downward wage rigidity, worker heterogeneity and
segmented markets, and show that it can generate similar asymmetries in unemployment, hiring,
and separations to the data.
Our definition of a ‘short-run NAIRU’ is similar to the concept discussed by Hall and Kudlyak (n.d.), who
argue that the natural rate of unemployment may vary cyclically and track the actual unemployment
rate during recoveries. Hall and Kudlyak (2022) discusses the ability of DMP-style models to generate

the slow persistent recovery in unemployment that occurs after recessions. Fujita and Ramey (2007)
show that adding vacancy adjustment costs to a DMP model can produce a more sluggish response
for vacancies and hiring after a downturn, slowing down the recovery in unemployment. Mercan,
Schoefer and Sedláček (2024) suggest a congestion mechanism, whereby new hires and incumbent
workers are imperfect substitutes in production, which also propagates unemployment after a shock.

This literature above generally abstracts from price rigidities and inflation dynamics. There is a
substantial literature incorporating labour-market frictions into New Keynesian models (see e.g.
Blanchard and Galí 2010). Recent examples include Moscarini and Postel-Vinay (2023), Faccini and
Melosi (2025), and Bloesch et al (2024). These papers focus on the role of on-the-job search and wage
competition between firms in inflation dynamics. We adopt a similar model for wage setting, drawing

on the tractable formulation in Bloesch et al (2024). But these papers use linear approximations.
The literature on downward nominal wage rigidity explores the extent to which it generates a
nonlinear Phillips curve and asymmetric cyclical dynamics (see e.g. Kim and Ruge-Murcia 2009;
Abbritti and Fahr 2013; Daly and Hobijn 2014; Benigno and Antonio Ricci 2011; Schmitt-Grohé
and Uribe 2022; Benigno and Eggertsson 2023; Benigno and Eggertsson 2024). Lepetit (2020)

also constructs a New Keynesian model with asymmetric unemployment dynamics, although the
asymmetry stems from the convex relationship between the job-finding rate and unemployment.
This literature does not model the labour market in as much detail (e.g. endogenous layoffs, on-the-
job search, job ladder) and does not seek to match the ‘plucking’ property in unemployment to the
same extent.
3 Fujita and Ramey (2012) also analyse versions of the DMP model with endogenous separations and on-the-job search,

but their focus is not on asymmetries in unemployment rate dynamics

### Page 7

4
2. Model
The model consists of firms, workers, and a policy rule. We start by outlining the firms’ problem
and optimal choices. Firms’ optimal decision-making drives most of the model’s mechanics. We then
discuss workers and the policy regime, which are modelled more simply. 5 contains a full list of
equilibrium conditions, as well as details of the calibration and solution method.
2.1 Firms
Firms make decisions around hiring, separations, and price- and wage-setting.

Hiring: Firms choose how many vacancies to post, V i t . These vacancies generate Mi t = qtV i t matches,
where qt is determined by a matching function and depends on aggregate matching efficiency and
labour market tightness. A proportion 1 − ϕ E t of these matches are unemployed searchers (where ϕ E t
is the share of unemployed searchers in total searchers, weighted by search intensity).
Firms must screen matches. Then the individual productivities of employed matches are revealed.

Firms make offers to those with productivities above some threshold xi t , which is 1−F i t of them (where
F i t = F(xi t ) and F is the CDF of the individual productivity distribution). For unemployed matches,
firms have no information about their individual productivity and make offers to all of them. This
feature proxies for the idea that firms may be more uncertain about the capabilities of unemployed
applicants.
Matches with offers choose whether to accept them; Ace t (W i t ) and employed matches and Acu t (W i t )

of unemployed matches do so, where W i t is the real wage paid by the firm. These job-acceptance
probabilities are characterised in the description of workers below.
The number of new hires is given by
Hi t = ϕ E t Mi t (1 − F i t )Ace t (W i t ) + (1 − ϕ E t )Mi t Acu t (W i t ).
Define Hi,u t = (1 − ϕ E t )Mi t Acu t (W i t ) as the number of hires from unemployment.
Recruiting costs include a per-vacancy cost and a per-match ‘screening’ cost:

Separations: There are two types of separation: quits and layoffs. Each period, Qt (W i t ) of firms’
existing employees quit (either to unemployment or to another job). Just as firms’ hiring rate is

increasing in the real wage they offer (via the job-acceptance probabilities Ace t (W i t ) and Acu t (W i t )),
their quits rate is decreasing in the real wage by the firm. This function is characterised in the section
below on workers.
Workers’ probability of being laid off depends on whether they are insecurely or securely attached.
Insecurely attached employees are all those who have just been hired out of unemployed in
the previous period. Before aggregate shocks are revealed, some portion of new hires from

### Page 8

5
unemployment, δ u, (i.e. who were hired the previous period), draw a new productivity from
distribution G. Those whose productivities are less than some threshold xi,m t−1 are laid off, which
is Gi t−1 of them (where Gi t = G(xi,m t−1) and G is the CDF of the distribution).4 All insecurely attached
employees who are not laid off then because securely attached (together with all those who have
been employed for more than one period).
After aggregate shocks are revealed and quitting decisions made, δ of securely attached employees

draw a new individual productivity. Firms then decide to lay off those whose productivities are less
than some threshold xi t , which is F i t of them (where F i t = F(xi t ) and F is the CDF of the individual
productivity distribution). The layoff probability for securely attached employees will be lower than
for insecurely attached employees both because δ < δ u and the distribution F has less mass at low
values than G.

Φ′ t = ϕ p (Πt − Π) .
This is a standard New Keynesian price Phillips curve.
2.2 Workers
All workers who are unemployed when shocks are revealed search for jobs. A share λ EE of employed
workers also search. The total number of searchers is then λ EE ˜Nt−1 + 1 − ˜Nt−1, and the effective
share of employed searchers is
ϕ E t = λ EE ˜Nt−1
λ EE ˜Nt−1 + 1 − ˜Nt−1 .
Those who enter unemployment after shocks are revealed, either because they are laid off or because

they quit to unemployment, do not search in that period.6
A share λ EU of employed workers consider quitting to unemployment.
6 This means that even if hiring costs went to zero, unemployment would remain above zero. If the newly unemployed
were allowed to search, this would raise the average number of searchers, especially in periods where layoffs are high.
Workers hired last period who are laid off before shocks are revealed can search. This is to avoid Hu t−1 or Nt−1 being a

state variable.

### Page 12

9
Workers’ only role in the model is to determine the labour supply schedule faced by firms. They do so
by determining the hiring and quits elasticities ε He t , ε Hu t and ε Q t via the functions Ace t (Wt ), Acu t (Wt )
and Q(Wt ).
Workers draw an idiosyncratic preferences for firms when they match, which they compare to any
wage differential. These preferences are drawn from a Gumbel distribution with parameter γ. If a
firm pays wage Wt , then the probability that a worker prefers a different firm that has made them an

offer is

2.3 Policy and aggregate variables
Labour market tightness: Tightness is

Using the DRW matching function gives match-finding and vacancy-matching rates
ft = (1 + θ −α t )− 1 α
qt = (1 + θ α t )− 1 α .
Monetary policy: Policy tries to implement a flexible inflation target, but is subject to various shocks
(e.g. it cannot fully offset all demand shocks immediately). Therefore:
ln Πt − ln Πtarget + γ p ( Nt − Ntarget ) = ln Mt

where Mt is an exogenous nominal demand shock.
2.4 Equilibrium, solution and calibration
We assume that firms have rational expectations, which implies that they understand the interaction
between nonlinearities in the economy and aggregate risk when forming their expectations. This
means, for example, that in the presence of downward nominal wage rigidity, firms will set lower
wages even in steady state, given the risk that it will be very costly for them to reduce wages if

negative shocks occur in the future.
We compute the global solution to the nonlinear model, so that we can explore how the interaction
between nonlinearities and aggregate risk affects expectations and dynamics. The state variables are
employment ˜Nt−1 (after insecurely attached employees are laid off), real wages Wt−1, the average
idiosyncratic productivity of employees ¯xt−1, and the aggregate productivity and demand shocks, At
and Mt .
The model is calibrated so that steady-state values for unemployment and key labour-market flows

are broadly similar to their values Australian data around 2025, as well as to match the standard
deviation of the unemployment rate over 1980 to 2019 and generate a similar positive skewness of
unemployment rate changes.
See Appendix 5 for more detail on the calibration and solution method.
3. How well can we match the dynamics of the labour market in the data?
3.1 Comparing the model to labour-market dynamics in the data
In this section, we compare the dynamics of unemployment and labour-market flows in the model

to Australian data. Figure 2 illustrates the path of transition rates and flows between labour-market
states in Australia since 1980. It shows that the UE rate is negative correlated with unemployment,
even though UE flows (as a proportion of the labour force) are positively correlated. UE flows increase
when the pool of unemployed is larger, but this increase is not enough for the UE rate to increase.

### Page 14

11
Figure 2: Labour-market transition rates and flows in Australia
Note: The UE and EU flows in the data are divided by the labour force size to make them comparable to
the model flows. The shaded regions represent contractions, which are identified using the algorithm in
Dupraz et al (2025).
Figure 3: Comparing recessions in the model and the data
Note: The UE and EU flows in the data are divided by the labour force size to make them comparable to the model

flows.
The figure also shows that the EU rate and EU flows are postively correlated with unemployment,
and the EE rate (i.e. job-to-job transition rate) is negatively correlated (at a cyclical frequency).
Figure 3 compares a stylised recession in our model to recessions in the data since 1980.9 The model
downturn is generated with a sequence of six demand shocks (over six months) calibrated to produce
a similar path for unemployment to the downturns in the data. The paths for transition rates and

flows from the model are broadly similar to the data. In particular, the UE rate falls while UE flows
increase, and the magnitude of all responses is comparable to the data.
9 We exclude the 2010-14 and 2022-25 upswings because they involved a gradual increase in unemployment rather than
a spike.

### Page 15

12
Next, we consider whether our model can reproduce the ‘plucking’ or asymmetric ‘sharp spike’ and
‘slow decline’ patterns we see in the unemployment rate data. One measure of this pattern is the
skewness of unemployment rate changes. In the data, this moment is quite positive, reflecting
frequent small decreases and occasional large increases (Table 1) (Sichel (1993) called this a
‘steepness’ asymmetry). Standard search-and-matching models tend to generate negative skewness

in unemployment changes (Ferraro 2023). In contrast, under the current calibration, simulations of
our model generate a substantially positive skewness of unemployment rate changes, although not
quite as large as in the data.
We can also compare other moments of the unemployment rate and moments of the unemployment-
to-employment (UE) and employment-to-unemployment (EU) transition rates in our model to the
data. The model generates somewhat higher variance and negative skewness in the UE rate, whereas

the UE rate is positively skewed in Australian data over this sample period. In US data, the UE rate
has higher variance and is negatively skewed (see Appendix A.3). The model matches the variance
and skewness of the EU rate well, but generates more skewness in changes in the EU rate than in
the data.

Unemployment rate UE rate EU rate

Skewness 1.12 0.59 -0.38 0.40 0.52 0.57
Skewness of changes 1.37 1.71 -0.74 -0.62 2.02 0.69
Notes: These moments use Australian data from 1980 to 2019, detrended using estimates from Ballantyne and Cusbert (2025). See
Appendix A.3 for moments using undetrended data and using US data.
3.2 Explaining the model’s dynamics
The IRFs in Figure 4 demonstrate why we can match these moments. The responses are
strongly nonlinear. Large negative shocks generate a large increase in the unemployment rate

(disproportionately more than small negative shocks), whereas positive shocks do not reduce it very
much. Unemployment recovers very slowly after an initial spike.
To explore why the model is able to generate these asymmetric dynamics, Table 2 compares the
model’s moments when three key frictions are removed, either one at a time or all together. First, the
downward nominal wage rigidity is removed by making wage adjustment costs symmetric. The next

column removes insecurely attached workers, so that all workers have the same layoff rate. The next
column removes convexity in recruiting costs, making them (almost) linear. The final column removes
all three frictions. In each case, we recalibrate the model so that it generates the same steady-state
unemployment rate and flows, and the same variance in unemployment. Figure 5 compares the
response of each version of the model to positive and negative demand shocks.

When all three frictions are removed (the last column in Table 2), the degree of asymmetry
declines and the skewness of unemployment rate changes is much lower. Unlike the standard DMP
model (Ferraro 2023), the skewness of unemployment changes remains positive, likely because
layoffs are still endogenous, which generates some asymmetry. Figure 5 shows that unemployment
recovers more rapidly, especially the UE rate. The value of a match is a forward-looking object,

determined largely by expected future surplus, and therefore rises quickly after the shock peaks.

### Page 16

13
Figure 4: Response to aggregate demand shocks
Note: The economy is initially at steady state.
When unemployment is high, the vacancy-filling rate is also high, so marginal recruiting costs fall.
Unemployed searchers make up a much larger proportion of total searchers, so for a given amount
of hiring, hires out of unemployment are higher.

Notes: The ‘Data’ column is based on detrended Australian data from 1980 to 2019. The ‘Model’ column includes all three frictions. The
next three columns remove one friction at a time. The last column removes all three frictions. See Table B2 for moments where
each friction is included on its own.
Below we explain the effect of each of the three frictions:10

### Page 17

14
• Convex recruiting costs: The convexity of recruiting costs moderates the response of hiring
to fluctuations in match value. Without this convexity, hiring responds much more to fluctuations
in match value, so the UE rate is too volatile (see the fifth column in Table 2 and the yellow lines
in Figure 5). After a negative shock, UE flows initially decline over the first year and the EU rate
is near steady state shortly after the initial spike. This reduces the skewness in unemployment

rate changes.
• Insecurely attached workers: The presence of insecurely attached workers is the most
significant contributor to the skewness in unemployment rate changes (see the fourth column in
Table 2 and the red lines in Figure 5). It does so in two ways. First, the layoff rate for securely
attached employees provides a highly nonlinear component to the EU rate. In steady state, very
few securely attached employees are laid off and the mass of employees near the layoff threshold

is small. Therefore, layoffs vary little for positive or small negative shocks. But when large negative
shocks occur, the mass of employees above the layoff threshold is much larger, so there is a
spike in layoffs. This helps generates asymmetric spikes in unemployment, although it does not
generate persistence in the EU rate once match values recover.11 But this is where the insecurely
attached component of layoffs helps. Their layoff rate is much higher, so when unemployment is

high and the flow of hires out of unemployment rises above steady state, the layoff rate also rises.
The unemployed may cycle through several short-term jobs before regaining secure employment.
This feature substantially slows the recovery in unemployment and helps generate persistence
and skewness in the EU rate.
• Asymmetric nominal wage stickiness: After a positive shock, nominal and real wages growth
increases, offsetting a lot of the increase in marginal revenue product. This moderates and

shortens the increase in match values for firms, dampening the incentive to increase employment
or any reduction in layoffs. In contrast, after a negative shock nominal wages growth does
not decrease very much. In the case of a negative demand shock, real wages growth actually
increases (due to the decline in inflation). This both amplifies and prolongs the decline in match
values for firms, which helps to generate both the asymmetric plucks and the slow recoveries in

unemployment (see the third column in Table 2 and the blue lines in Figure 5).
4. Phillips curve and NAIRU
4.1 Nonlinear Phillips curve
The model implies that the relationship between inflation and unemployment (i.e. the Phillips
curve) is nonlinear and state-dependent. Figures 6 and 7 show this relationship by plotting inflation
against unemployment conditional on different initial values for unemployment or real wages.12 The
Phillips curve is much steeper at low rates of unemployment, but this steepening occurs at higher

10 Dupraz et al (2025) also include insecurely attached workers and downward nominal wage rigidities to generate
asymmetry in unemployment dynamics.
11 The layoff rate for securely attached employees moves contemporaneously with match values, and is therefore only as
persistent as match values.
12 Specifically, we vary the policy/demand shock variable and plot the maximum deviation of inflation from target against
the unemployment rate for each value of this shock. This accounts for the dynamic relationship between inflation and

unemployment in the model, which is largely not contemporaneous. The peak unemployment response tends to be
quite lagged, because the model includes many labour-market frictions, but inflation responds very rapidly, because
expectations are forward-looking and based on full information. Introducing features like indexation, backward-looking
expectations, inattention or imperfect information could slow the inflation response.

### Page 18

15
Figure 5: Response to aggregate demand shocks with different versions of model
Note: There are two shocks for each model: the largest positive and largest negative shocks from Figure 4. The
economy is initially at steady state.
Figure 6: Phillips curve given initial unemployment
Note: The Phillips curves are constructed by varying the demand shock and plotting the inflation and
unemployment outcomes that it traces out. The dashed vertical line is steady-state unemployment.

unemployment rates when the initial unemployment rate is higher. When the initial level of real wages
is higher, the Phillips curve shifts upwards.
4.2 Short-run NAIRU
Figures 6 and 7 show that the unemployment rate consistent with maintaining inflation at target
(i.e. the intersection of the Phillips curves with the x-axis) is state-dependent. At steady state, it is

### Page 19

16
Figure 7: Phillips curve given initial level of real wages
Note: The Phillips curves are constructed by varying the demand shock and plotting the inflation and
unemployment outcomes that it traces out. The dashed vertical line is steady-state unemployment.
4.5 percent under our calibration of the model. But if unemployment or real wages increase, then
the Phillips curve shifts and the one-year-ahead unemployment forecast consistent with at-target

inflation increases.
We can therefore construct a ‘short-run NAIRU’ concept in our model. We define it as follows: if
policymakers wanted to eliminate any deviations in inflation from target stemming excessive or
insufficient demand in the labour market, what unemployment rate at a one-year horizon would
be required? This is essentially the intercept with the x-axis in Figures 6 and 7. At steady state, it
coincides with the long-run NIARU (i.e. the intersection of the steady-state Phillips curve in black

with the x-axis).
Figure 8 uses the model to calculate the historical path of the ‘short-run NAIRU’ in Australia from
1980 to 2025. It is calculated by: (i) calculating the sequence of demand shocks that in our model
replicates the historical path of the unemployment rate;13 (ii) using the model to simulate the inflation
generated by this sequence of demand shocks, which we define as the ‘labour-market’ component of
inflation;14 (iii) given the state of the economy in each period, calculating the sequence of demand

shocks consistent with forecast inflation being at target; and (iv) calculating the the one-year-ahead
forecast for the unemployment rate given that sequence of shocks and defining it as as the ‘one-
year-ahead NAIRU’ for that period.15
13 For simplicity, we are assuming that historical unemployment has been driven by only demand shocks. It is likely that
other shocks also drove some of the variation in the unemployment rate.
14 This simulated inflation series is in Figure B1.

15 We are ignoring here potential interactions between supply-driven inflation and the labour market. For example, an
inflationary supply shock might make it easier to reduce real wages, allowing the labour-market-driven component of
inflation to fall without as much of an increase in unemployment.

### Page 20

17
Figure 8: Short-run NAIRU
Note: The long-run NAIRU estimate is from Ballantyne and Cusbert (2025).
Figure 8 shows that the short-run NAIRU varies cyclically much more than the long-run NAIRU.16
When unemployment is above trend, the short-run NAIRU tends to increase and track the actual
unemployment rate. But there is some asymmetry: in the pre-GFC period when unemployment fell
below trend, the short-run NAIRU did not decline. In the recent period, the short-run NAIRU was

above the long-run NAIRU, even though actual unemployment was below it. In a tight labour market,
unemployment declines, which shifts the Phillips curve to the left, but real wages rise, which shifts
the Phillips curve up. The effect on the short-run NAIRU is ambiguous.
4.3 Long-run NAIRU
Although the dynamics of the model describe only cyclical fluctuations in unemployment, the model’s
long-run properties can be used to consider the determinants of the long-run NAIRU (i.e. the NAIRU

concept that is most commonly estimated by central banks using latent-variable models with a unit-
root NAIRU process, e.g. (Ballantyne and Cusbert 2025)).
The deterministic steady-state unemployment rate in the model is determined by parameters such
as recruiting costs, on-the-job search effort, and firms’ labour market monopsony power determine
the long-run NAIRU in the model. But this concept is not exactly analogous to the long-run NAIRU,
because it assumes that agents believe there will be no shocks in the future. The long-run NAIRU

is instead the stochastic steady-state unemployment rate, which is the unemployment rate in the
absence of shocks but assuming agents still believe shocks will occur in the future according to their
specified distribution.
Since we compute the global nonlinear solution to the model with rational expectations and aggregate
risk, we can consider how changes in cyclical properties of the model (e.g. shock volatility, price and
16 Hall and Kudlyak (n.d.) also discuss the concept of a short-run NAIRU that fluctuates cyclically.

### Page 21

18
Figure 9: Effect of parameters on the long-run NAIRU
Note: The dashed vertical line represents the calibration in the baseline model.
wage stickiness, monetary policy) affect the long-run NAIRU via agents’ response to expectations
and risk. Figure 9 shows how the long-run NAIRU changes when certain parameters are varied.
Increasing the weight on unemployment in the policy rule increases the long-run NAIRU. This may
reflect a kind of level-variance trade off: if policy stabilises labour demand to a greater extent, the

downward nominal wage rigidity will bind less often. Firms understand this decline in the risk of
incurring wage adjustment costs in the future, and are therefore more willing to set higher real
wages in steady state, which reduces match values and raises the level of unemployment in steady
state.
Increases in the stickiness of wages or prices also increases the long-run NAIRU. If firms are less
able to raise wages in response to positive shocks, they will set higher real wages in steady state,

which increases steady-state unemployment. If prices are stickier, then nominal wages do not need
to decline as much in response to negative demand shocks, which means that downward nominal
wage rigidities bind less often. Again, firms will set higher real wages in steady state and employ
fewer workers.
This results may be affected by the calibration of the model. For example, a greater variance in
productivity shocks compared to demand shocks could change how parameters affect the long-run

NAIRU.
5. Conclusion
We construct a model of the labour-market that can generate the asymmetric dynamics of
unemployment in the data (given symmetric shocks) and can be used to study the implications for
inflation. We find that endogenous layoffs, a ‘slippery’ job ladder, downward nominal wage rigidity,
and convex recruiting costs are the key frictions that produce the asymmetric dynamics. The model
can generate a substantially positive skewness in unemployment changes, which is a feature of the

data but not most existing structural models of the labour market. The model implies that the short-
run Phillips curve is nonlinear and state-dependent, and that the short-run NAIRU fluctuates cyclically

### Page 22

19
and asymmetrically, tending to increase when unemployment is above trend, but may not decline
when unemployment is below trend. The long-run NAIRU is affected by cyclical factors (e.g. shock
volatility, nominal rigidities, policy regime), because the nonlinear cyclical dynamics and aggregate
risk interact to affect firms’ expectations and employment decisions even in the absence of shocks.

A.2 Calibration
Labour flows and stocks: In steady state, hires from unemployment equal quits to unemployment
and layoffs:
Hu = ( Qu + ˜F s) N + Gδ uHu.
Therefore:
Hu = Qu + ˜F s
1 − Gδ u N
EU = Qu + ˜F s
1 − Gδ u
We set G = 0.8 and δ u = 1. Then we set steady state values for N, Qu, and ˜F s so that the stochastic
steady states for N, UE, and EU match their values in the data (somewhere between 2019 values
and 2025 values, which means around 4-4.5% unemployment, 20-25% UE rate, and 1% EU rate).

We assume the quit-to-unemployment rate is around 0.2%, so the layoff rate is around 0.8%, and
the secure layoff rate is around 0.05%.17
We set the steady-state EE rate to try to match its value in the data (around 1-1.5%).
Match productivity distributions: We set δ = 0.1, so that 10% of employees draw a new
productivity each month (and can be laid off). Setting δ too high means that match productivities
are not very persistent and so have little effect on expected match surplus. Setting δ too low means

that layoffs cannot rise much.
We assume that firms do not know the productivity of unemployed matches, so they accept all of
them (F u = 0).
The match productivity distributions F, F u and G are all truncated lognormal. We set the mean of the
main distribution µx = 0.1 and the mean of the distribution for unemployed matches µx,u = −0.5.
The wage of newly hired unemployed workers is a fixed amount lower than the aggregate wage:
Wt −W u t = ξ = 0.5. The standard deviation of the distribution that new hires out of unemployment

draw from after their first period, G, is set to be 3 times larger than the standard deviations of the
main distribution F and the distribution for unemployed matches before they are hired F u.18 The
remaining parameters of the distributions (i.e. the truncation point, the standard deviation, and the
mean of G) are then determined by the steady-state flows.
Matching function and recruiting costs: The matching function elasticity α = 0.5. The convexity

parameter for recruiting costs is set χ = 4. We assume no training cost, κ T = 0, and then κ and κ v
are calibrated so that 20% of total recruiting costs are per-vacancy, while 80% are per-match.
Note that C ˜N,t+1 = −χ
Ct+1
˜Nt and (1 + χ) Ct Mt = CV,t qt + CM,t = He t Mt λ N t + Hu t Mt λ Hu t , so C ˜N,t+1 =
− χ 1+χ He t+1λ N t+1+Hu t+1λ Hu t+1 ˜Nt .
17 Assuming a lot of insecure layoffs and few secure layoffs in steady state (i.e. high G) helps to generate skew in

unemployment changes and slow recoveries. In the Dupraz, Nakamura and Steinsson model, the exogenous separation
rate for insecurely-attached workers is 96%, compared to 0.02% for securely-attached workers. They say this is the
order of magnitude required to generate slow recoveries.
18 A higher variance for G seems to help generate skewness in unemployment changes.

ε He = γ 1 2, ε Hu = γ bγ bγ + 1 , ε Q = −γ λ EE f (1 − F)Ace′ + λ EU Acu′ λ EE f (1 − F)Ace + λ EU (1 − Acu) .
The steady-state flow rates determine λ EE and λ EU . We set b = 0.75. In steady state:
W N = ε He Heλ N + ε Hu Huλ Hu W W − ξ − ε QQ(1 − δ F) ˜Nλ N + ε QQ(1 − δ ) ˜N(xa − ˜L ˜N )λ L
λ N = xa −W − βCN
1 − β (1 − S)
λ Hu = (1 − β (1 − S)Gδ u)λ N + ξ + Gδ uxaλ ˜L + (xa,u − xa)λ L + ((1 − G)xa,m − xa,u)δ uλ ˜L

CN = − χ 1 + χ Heλ N + Huλ Hu ˜N .
As γ → ∞ (i.e. idiosyncratic preferences become unimportant), recruitment and separation elasticities
become infinite, and W approaches a weighted average of xa, xa,u and xa,m, while λ N converges to
slightly above 0 (if λ Hu = λ N ), then λ N → 0). As γ → 0 (i.e. idiosyncratic preferences entirely
determine job choices), recruitment and separation elasticities go to zero, and W → 0. The wage
markdown expression differs from the standard ε 1+ε because the elasticities are multiplied by S

1−β
(
1− 1 1+χ S ) . This is because the elasticity applies only to hires in a given period (S), but firms
account for the surplus over the expected lifetime of the match.
We set γ = 100, so the wage markdown is very small. This helps to generate sufficiently large
responses in unemployment, especially in layoffs.19
For wage adjustment costs, we need to choose a function ϕ w(Πw). We set ϕ w to 2500 when nominal
wages growth is above ¯Πw = (1/1.005)(1/12) (i.e. 0.5 percent below steady-state inflation) and

20000 when it is below ¯Πw.
Price setting: We set θ = 7.87 (a standard value in the New Keynesian literature). Price stickiness
ϕ p = 2500.
Policy: We set γ p = 1 48 , which implies that policymakers place four times as much weight on
annualised inflation compared to unemployment in their policy response.
Shocks: The shocks are AR(1) processes with persistence 0.96. The standard deviations are scaled
so that the variance of unemployment matches its variance in the data.

A.3 Solution method
The state variables are ˜Nt−1, Wt−1, ˜Lt−1 and the exogenous shocks At and Mt .
We use policy function iteration to solve for the equilibrium. We approximate λ
˜N
t , λ
˜L
t , Φ′ t Πt , Ψt Πw t
and E p t = IEt Φ′ t+1Πt+1 Yt+1 Yt are piecewise linear functions of the state.
Ideally, we would like to choose variables that can be well-approximated as piecewise linear functions
of the state. This is why we choose to approximate e.g. Ψt Πw t instead of Πw t . Expectations would work

19 Dupraz, Nakamura and Steinsson similarly have only a very small wage markdown. It is well known in the literature that
search and matching models can struggle to match unemployment data unless match surplus is very small.

### Page 27

24
well, because averaging over possible future states ‘smoothes out’ nonlinearities. The expectations
in the model are λ
˜N
t , λ
˜L
t and
E p t = IEt Φ′ t+1Πt+1 Yt+1 Yt
Ew t = IEt Ψ′ t+1ΠW t+1 Wt+1Nt+1 Wt Nt .
Given values for these variables, all period t variables are determined. But solving the intratemporal
equilibrium conditions to get period t variables requires solving a complicated system of nonlinear
equations. So we have chosen variables which mostly allow us to solve the intratemporal conditions

directly.20
Similarly, we could also instead just approximate four variables, which would likely require fewer
iterations and could be more accurate. But approximating five makes it easier to solve the
intratemporal equilibrium conditions, so each iteration is faster.
20 There is one equation that we cannot solve directly. To avoid using a nonlinear solver within each iteration, we first solve
it on a grid before starting the policy function iteration. Then we use this approximate solution in each iteration.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
OCTOBER CPI PREVIEW
Inflationary pressure fades as we move through year-end
17 November 2026
Key points
• October CPI to rise 0.41% with the annual 
pace lifting to 4.8%yr. The seasonally 
adjusted CPI lifts 0.7%mth, a small step 
up from the 0.6% increase in September.  
• October Trimmed Mean (TM) is set 
to gain 0.42%mth lifting the annual 
pace to 3.7%yr from 3.6%yr. 
• There is a small downside revision 
from our October 19th estimate for 
the December quarter CPI to 1.2%qtr 
while the TM remains 0.9%qtr.  
• The December quarter will represent the 
peak in Australian inflationary pressure. 
Our March quarter CPI remains 0.4%qtr 
and the TM is unchanged at 0.7%qtr. 
• We now see TM inflation dipping below 
3% by Q3 2027 and be well within the 
RBA’s target band by end 2027 at 2.7%yr.
• Demand remains the larger driver of 
inflation, but the recent pick-up in 
trimmed mean inflation appears to 
have been largely supply-driven.

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Inflation accelerated in September...
The CPI rose 0.8% in the month of September, stronger than 
Westpac’s 0.6% estimate and the market’s expectation of 
0.7%. The annual pace picked up from 4.0%yr to 4.3%yr. The 
seasonally adjusted CPI rose 0.6% in September highlighting 
some positive seasonality in the month. 
Key factors driving the headline monthly result were: auto 
fuels (9.4%mth); domestic holiday travel & accommodation 
(8.0%mth); international holiday travel & accommodation 
(7.2%mth); and property rates & charges (5.5%mth). There 
were modest offsets from: women’s garments (–3.1%mth); 
household textiles (–6.6%mth); and accessories (–4.1%mth).
The more important underlying measure, the Trimmed Mean 
(TM), gained 0.3% in September, spot on both Westpac and 
market expectations with the annual pace lifting from 3.5%yr 
(revised from 3.6%yr) to 3.6%yr. The six-month annualised 
pace is now 4.0%yr the fastest in the short history of this 
series.
Overall, the breadth of inflation moderated for a second 
consecutive month. The share of the basket running above 
3%yr fell from 40% to 35%, while the share below 2%yr 
rose from 27% to 32%. Meanwhile, our replication of the San 
Francisco Fed’s inflation shock momentum index moved into 
positive territory after trending higher since April. Around 
14% of the CPI basket, on an expenditure-weighted basis, 
experienced positive inflation shocks in September, driven 
by holiday travel, fuel, and clothing & footwear. This was 
partly offset by negative shocks across 7% of the basket, 
concentrated in food & non-alcoholic beverages. For full 
analysis of the September CPI, see “Inflation accelerating in 
the run up to Christmas”.
... but is set to peak in October.
Looking ahead to October, early indicators were mixed. The 
NAB business survey suggested firms continued to absorb 
a meaningful share of higher input costs, with purchase cost 
growth edging up to 2.4%qtr (+0.1ppt from September), while 
retail price growth slowed sharply to 0.2%qtr (–0.8ppts), 
partly reflecting some moderation in labour cost growth 
(see here). Meanwhile, consumers’ year-ahead inflation 
expectations rose to 5.3% in October, with this rate holding 
steady in November. Fuel prices provided a more pronounced 
source of inflationary pressure, with retail petrol and diesel 
prices jumping 8.7% and 5.3%, respectively, in October to 
average $2.40/l and $2.82/l as global oil prices approached a 
local peak.
October to mark the peak in inflation
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Neha Sharma, Economist

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
We expect the October CPI to rise 0.4% (0.41% to 2dp), lifting
annual inflation to 4.8%, which we expect to mark the peak.
On a seasonally adjusted basis, we estimate a stronger 0.7%
increase (0.68% to 2dp). For the monthly trimmed mean, we

expect a 0.4% rise (0.42% to 2dp), nudging the annual pace up
to 3.7%. On a 3m/3m basis, the TM is tracking a 1.0% increase
in October. While conceptually different from the quarterly
trimmed mean, this measure nonetheless provides a useful
guide to near-term underlying inflation momentum. For market
services excluding volatiles & holiday travel, we have pencilled
in a 0.3% rise, lifting annual inflation to 4.0% from 3.8%,
which had marked a ten-month low.

Transport is expected to make the largest contribution
(+0.35ppts), almost entirely reflecting auto fuel (+0.32ppts).
Smaller contributions are expected from clothing & footwear
(+0.06ppts), household goods & services (+0.05ppts), and
meals out & takeaway (+0.04ppts). The main offset comes
from recreation & culture (–0.18ppts), driven entirely by
holiday travel (–0.18ppts), while fruit & vegetables, utilities
and several other components provide only marginal offsets.

Key details for the month of October
Food & non-alcoholic beverages are expected to rise 0.2% in
October, lifting annual inflation to 3.3%. Strength in the month
is concentrated in meals out & takeaway, where we expect
prices to rise 0.6%mth and 4.3%yr. We have allowed for a
greater-than-usual degree of price resetting as businesses
adjust to the card surcharging ban.
Housing inflation is expected to rise 0.1% in October, with the
annual pace increasing to 6.2% reflecting base effects from

electricity. Across the major components:
• Rents are expected to rise 0.2%mth and 3.5%yr, with the
late-September indexation of the Commonwealth Rent
Assistance flowing through more broadly in October. Our
seasonally adjusted estimate is stronger at 0.4%mth.
• New dwelling inflation has been a key source of downside
surprise in recent months. We expect a 0.2%mth rise
in October, which would see the annual pace moderate
to 4.5%. The downturn in established home prices has

deepened into year-end (see here), which should weigh on
new dwelling inflation over the months ahead. Our current
estimates point to a trough of around 1.3%yr by late-2027.
• Maintenance & repair costs are expected rise a solid
0.9%mth, taking annual inflation to 3.5%, while gas & other
household fuels provide the main offset, falling 2.5%mth.
Transport is expected to be the main source of upward
pressure in October, rising 2.9%mth and lifting to 11.6%yr.

This largely reflects an 8.2%mth jump in auto fuel prices
following the rise in global oil prices through the month. Motor
vehicle prices and spare parts are also expected to increase, by
0.4%mth and 1.6%mth respectively.

Recreation & culture is expected to provide the key offset, 
falling 1.5%mth, driven by a 3.0%mth decline in holiday travel. 
We also expect some pull-back across components exposed 
to the AI trade, including audio, visual & computing equipment 
(–0.2%mth) and games, toys & hobbies (–0.3%mth).
Underlying inflation holds momentum
As noted above, our October TM estimate is also 0.4%mth, a step 
up from 0.3% in September taking the annual pace to 3.7%yr from 
3.6%yr and lifting the six month annualised pace to 4.2%yr, from 
4.0%yr, the fastest pace in the short history of the series. Rents 
and dwellings continue to underpin the TM estimates. 
Upside risks to October baseline ...
We view the risks around the October CPI outcome as slightly 
tilted to the upside. Our estimates already allow for a greater 
degree of price resetting in areas such as meals out & takeaway 
and personal and household services, but there is a risk that this 
adjustment proves broader than we have assumed. Increases in 
fuel levy surcharges in early October are another source of upside 
risk, including increases by Woolworths and Australia Post, with 
the latter nearly doubling its surcharge between September and 
October. The key downside risk remains housing, particularly 
new dwelling prices, which have surprised to the downside in 
recent months and could continue to do so as the housing market 
downturn deepens. Trade notifications data also point to some 
further downside risk, with builders on average announcing 
smaller price changes than in prior months.
... but momentum to ease in November ...
We have a preliminary estimate for the November CPI of 
–0.1%mth which will see the annual pace ease back to 4.6%yr. 
Auto fuel moved lower through November to be down –2.1% in 
the month with seasonal falls in garments, health, furniture & 
household appliances and holiday travel offsetting some strength 
in food, housing, communication and financial services. 
Seasonally adjusted the CPI is estimated to lift 0.1% in November, 
highlighting the seasonal weakness in the month, while our TM 
estimate is for a 0.2% lift seeing the annual pace ease a touch to 
3.6%yr while the six month annualised pace drops from 4.2%yr 
to 3.9%yr. This moderation is expected to continue into December 
with a preliminary TM estimate of 0.2%mth/3.6%yr. 
The following page has a table with monthly profiles for the CPI, 
seasonally adjusted CPI and the TM out to July 2027. By July 
2027 we now expect an annual pace of 3.3%yr for the CPI and 
seasonally adjusted CPI while our estimate for the TM is 3.0%yr. 
More significantly, the six month annualised pace of the TM is 
estimated to be down to 2.7%yr by June 2027, well within the 
RBA’s target band. We have also included tables breaking down 
the monthly components of the CPI through the December and 
March quarters. 
... with December quarter now the peak ...
Our December quarter CPI estimate has been revised down 
slightly to 1.2%qtr, a modest 0.05ppt revision, with the annual 
pace lifting from 3.9%yr to 4.6%yr. The seasonally adjusted

CPI gains 1.4% in the December quarter highlighting normally
December is a seasonally soft quarter. Our TM estimate for
Q3 has a small downward revision of 0.05ppt to 0.85% at two
decimal place but this rounds up to our published estimate of
0.9%qtr. For now that suggests downside risk for our TM

estimate as getting the quarter to round to 0.8% is much
easier than getting it to round up to 1.0%. We also note
auto fuel prices falling faster than expected which may not
only result in downside risks to out CPI estimate but also our
TM estimate due to less pass through pressure.
... with inflation moderating through 2027 ...
Westpac’s preliminary March quarter CPI estimate is just 0.4%.
Driving most of this outcome is the 7.7% decline in autofuel

(–0.29ppt) and a 3.8% fall in holiday travel (–0.23ppt) being
offset by rising food (1.0%, 0.18ppt), housing (0.7%, 0.15ppt),
health (2.6%, 0.18ppt) and education (3.5%, 0.17ppt). The
March quarter does not have a lot in the way of net seasonally
with the seasonally adjusted estimate up 0.5%. Our underlying
TM measure of inflation is estimated to rise 0.7% in the March
quarter taking the annual pace down to 3.5% while the six
month annualised pace dips from 3.8%yr to 3.2%yr.

... as a disinflationary impulse kicks in.
We have for some time expected auto fuel prices to peak in
October, 240 cpl for petrol and 282 cpl for diesel, then to
trend lower from there. Our November estimates are currently
sitting on 237 cpl and 279 cpl respectively trending down to
186 cpl for petrol and 196 cpl for diesel by December 2027.
This represents a 12.7% fall in auto fuel through the first half
of 2027 subtracting 0.42ppt off the CPI over the same period

of time. For a detailed description on how we constructed the
auto fuels profile please see: “Persistent energy pressures
have further to run”. At this stage we have not adjusted our
Brent forecast in line with the current correction underway
(see the November Market Outlook for more details why) as
we expect this to be a volatile ride. Should the current correc-
tion continue, auto fuel will represent a larger drag on the CPI
and potentially the Trimmed Mean.

Critical to our expected moderation in core inflation is a step
down in dwelling price inflation on the back of falling estab-
lished home prices. From a 1.0%qtr increase in Q3 2026 we
see dwelling price increasing by 0.4% in Q4 2026, and then
0.5% in Q1 and 0.4%qtr in Q2 before dropping to 0.2%qtr in
Q3 and Q4. This will see dwelling price inflation in the CPI
drop from 5.4%yr at June 2026 to 2.3%yr by June 2027 then

down to 1.3%yr by December 2027.
At least to the end of this year there is still a high degree of
uncertainty about how much businesses will be able to pass
through upstream cost pressures. The latest NAB business sur-
vey continues to highlight robust input price pressure but also

that firms are struggling to pass on those increases.
For businesses, it is almost a now or never opportunity to raise
prices. We expect that through 2027, as energy prices ease
and the domestic economy slows under the weight of higher
interest rates and falling established home prices, households
will be much more resistant to price increases especially if dis-
cretionary household spending softens by more than expected.
That said, these headwinds may be at least partially offset by

the stronger starting point for household incomes, which has
proven more resilient than previously anticipated, and a possi-
ble bounce back in energy prices due to renewed conflict in the
Middle East.
Supply or demand driven inflation?
Inflation has steadily drifted away from the RBA’s target over
recent quarters. While the demand side is the larger driver of
inflation currently, most of the recent pick-up in TM inflation
appears to be from the supply side, and the more detailed

breakdown may understate how much.
There are several ways to split inflation into supply and de-
mand. One is to estimate how much of the rise a standard de-
mand-driven model can explain and attribute the rest to supply
(see here). Run through to Q3 2026, this approach puts most of
the recent pick-up in TM inflation down to supply. Without it,
trimmed mean would have risen from around 3.0%yr to 3.2%yr
over the year to Q3 2026, rather than the 2.9%yr to 3.7%yr

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
rise we actually saw. The supply contribution is moderate,
though, and well below its Covid-era peak. This analysis also
reveals that most of the disinflation through late-2023 and into
early-2025 reflected supply pressures unwinding, while de-
mand-driven inflation remained stickier, persisting above 3%yr.

Another is the San Francisco Fed approach, which the RBA has
also replicated (see here). The attribution is that if prices rise
and quantities fall, it is supply; if both rise, it is demand. We run
it to Q2 2026, as Q3 volume data are not yet available. Like the
first approach, it finds that demand accounts for most of infla-
tion (headline in this case). However, unlike the first approach,
it shows supply contributions easing a little.
A closer look at the categories classified as demand tells a

more nuanced story. Utilities, health and insurance all feature,
and their prices are largely shaped by government policy or are
not the categories that usually come to mind when people talk
about demand-driven inflation, and they are not very sensitive
to interest rates. You can look at electricity as a clear example
where prices in recent history have been set by government
policies (the introduction of, and then removal of, cost of living
rebates as well as policies to decarbonise the economy) or ex-

ternal supply shock (rising gas prices pushing up the marginal
cost of generation).
Categories more commonly thought of discretionary do show
up on the demand side, with recreation & culture (excluding
travel) and meals out & takeaway being the key ones. The
drawback of this sort of decomposition is that it labels cate-
gories where prices and quantities move together as demand,
even when this may reflect the ripple effects of a supply shock
elsewhere, a point our Chief Economist Luci Ellis detailed in a

note in 2024 (see In a world gone bananas, don’t squash the
other fruit). Some of this may be occurring now. In particular,
the analysis is likely not capturing the shift in spending towards
these categories resulting from travel disruptions associated
with the Middle East conflict (travel services itself was classi-
fied as a supply-side contributor in Q2). As travel normalises,
some of the “demand” contribution from recreation & culture
and meals out & takeaway should therefore fade over coming

quarters.
We would also acknowledge the work of Tim Toohey and his
analysis “The RBA has an identity problem”.

### Page 2

Last week was brutal. All 11 S&P 500 sectors fell sharply. The S&P 500
dropped 3.7% on Friday alone. The index has fallen for seven days in a
row, its longest losing streak since February 2020. It is up just 1.0% ytd.
The Nasdaq is on its longest weekly losing streak since 2008. The S&P
500 Semiconductors index is down 28.1% mtd.
Last week, we compared November's Friday the 13th S&P 500 close to
August 3, when the index first rose to the same level. We noted that while

the index was relatively calm over that period, there was lots of turbulence
below the surface in the S&P 500 sectors. The calm broke last week. The
S&P 500 is down 9.1% since August 3, and only three sectors remain
above water. Health Care (OW) and Consumer Staples (UW) are up 9.2%
and 8.6% (chart). Energy (OW) is up 2.8% and remains the best sector
performer ytd at a 34.8% gain. Communication Services (MW) is down
19.7%, with Information Technology (MW) and Industrials (OW) down

15.2% and 14.7%.

### Page 3

So far, the selloff has been driven more by increasingly bearish valuation
multiples than by analysts' consensus earnings estimates, which remain
bullish. Let's have a closer look at Information Technology,
Communication Services, and Financials:
(1) Information Technology. Investors don't believe the analysts'
earnings forecasts. Analysts expect IT earnings to grow 58.4% this year
and 48.4% next year. They expect Semiconductors earnings to grow
114.6% and 77.8% this year and next and Semiconductor Equipment

earnings to grow 36.3% and 51.3%. Investors are not paying for that. The
forward P/E of the sector is down 41.9% ytd, while forward earnings is up
71.0% (chart). The stock price index is down 0.7%, after rising over 30%
in early October.

### Page 4

Semiconductors show the gap most clearly. The industry accounts for
54.9% of IT's forward earnings and 38.0% of its market cap. Its forward
P/E has fallen to 9.4, well below the S&P 500's 16.6, and is near its 2010-
11 lows (chart).
Semiconductor Equipment is no different. Its forward earnings is up 89.7%
ytd, while its forward P/E is down 41.8% (chart).
Application Software is the exception. Analysts cut its 2027 earnings
growth forecast to 9.5% from about 17% in August (chart). It is down

28.6% ytd, the worst in the Information Technology industry.

### Page 5

We lowered our sector recommendation to market-weight late last year.
We are sticking with it because we believe the AI buildout will survive CEO
Jensen Huang's resignation from Nvidia.
(2) Communication Services. Meta and Alphabet make up about 40% of
the sector. Both have been caught up in the AI selloff. Meta is down 28%
from its ytd peak, and Alphabet is down 34%. The sector is down 15.5%
ytd, the worst of all 11 S&P 500 sectors. Forward earnings is up 21.5%

ytd, while the forward P/E is down 26.0% (chart).
Analysts expect Interactive Media & Services revenues to grow 25.8% in
2027. Yet they expect earnings to fall 14.2% (chart). Much of that reflects
a base effect from this year's mark-to-market gains posted by Alphabet.

### Page 6

The forward profit margin has also dropped to 26.4% from almost 32%
this summer, partly reflecting the reversal of the mark-to-market impact
next year.
We retain our market-weight rating on Communication Services because a
slower pace of the AI buildout should boost the profitability and cash flow
of the Magnificent-7 including Alphabet and Meta.
(3) Financials. Investors are treating parts of the sector as an AI trade,
which has dragged the whole group down. Investment Banking &

Brokerage fell 11.7% this week, trading down in step with IT. Last week,
we noted how the sector's forward P/E had fallen to 15.2. It is now 14.2,
down from 16.4 at the end of 2025 (chart). Forward earnings is still up
14.1% ytd.

### Page 7

Insurance has been the shelter from the storm. The Property & Casualty
Insurance price index is near a record high, and Insurance Brokers trades
11.8% above its 200-day moving average. Property & Casualty
Insurance's forward profit margin of 13.7% is the highest since 2007
(chart). Analysts expect earnings to grow 11.9% this year but to decline
8.4% next year, so the margin is likely peaking.
Consumer Finance is especially at risk. The industry’s price index is down

21.6% ytd, with a forward P/E that has dropped to 10.2. Yet analysts
expect that industry’s earnings to grow 11.3% this year and 11.4% next
year (chart). The industry is getting cheaper even as earnings grow.
Investors worry that Washington will cap credit card interest rates next
year.

### Page 8

Analysts expect Financials' earnings growth to slow to 7.4% next year
from 16.9% this year. Some de-rating makes sense. But with the sector
trading at 14.2 times forward earnings, we think the market has already
priced in the slowdown. We retain our overweight rating on Financials.
💡

長幅度大於收入成長幅度，導致效率比率變差。儘
管信貸品質仍健康無虞，但仍有部份銀行對潛在風
險展現謹慎態度，因而提高壞帳準備金。

## Document 532: 从美股到黄金、铜：全球大清算已近尾声？

Source PDF: all report pdf/从美股到黄金、铜：全球大清算已近尾声？.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-26
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

全球市场持续遭遇抛售，但纳指100、⻩⾦和铜价相继出现⼤级别看涨锤形K线，卖压衰竭
信号显现。⾼盛预计，系统性策略未来⼀周仍将抛售约1000亿美元股票，但12⽉初有望转
为温和买⼊。与此同时，韩国半导体估值跌⾄历史低位，⻩⾦周线超卖程度达到2022年以
来最⾼⽔平，市场触底信号逐渐增多。
摘要：
市场正出现多个潜在触底信号。纳斯达克100指数、⻩⾦和铜价在同⼀交易⽇相继出现⼤级别看
涨锤形K线，近期持续抛售后，部分资产开始显现卖压衰竭迹象。与此同时，⾼盛模型显⽰，系
统性策略的抛售压⼒有望在未来数周逐步减弱，若股市企稳，12⽉初甚⾄可能转为温和买⼊。
当前市场仍处于⾼度紧张状态。VIX虽从盘中⾼点回落，但仍在28附近，叠加⻓假周末临近，投
资者对冲需求依然⾼企。⾼盛估计，系统性宏观策略过去⼀周已抛售约700亿美元全球股票，未
来⼀周预计还将追加约1000亿美元卖盘。
不过，市场的关键变化在于卖压可能逐步⻅顶。⾼盛模型显⽰，在基准情景下，系统性卖出有
望于12⽉初转为温和买⼊；与此同时，韩国半导体估值降⾄历史低位，铜价接近关键⽀撑，⻩

⾦则在重要技术位附近强势反弹。多重信号交织之下，全球市场是否正在迎来阶段性拐点，值
得关注。
纳指反转信号显现，⾼盛预计系统性卖压逐步消退
纳斯达克100指数形成⼤级别锤形K线。这⼀形态出现在近期⼤幅下跌之后，通常意味着盘中抛
压得到明显消化，卖⽅⼒量可能趋于衰竭，短期反转信号开始显现。若后续出现确认性上涨，
市场逼空⾏情可能进⼀步展开。
期权市场也显⽰投资者情绪已明显转向悲观。⾼盛基于看涨与看跌期权成交量、看涨期权偏斜
及现货与波动率相关性构建的综合指标显⽰，纳斯达克及半导体ETF的期权隐含仓位已降⾄过去
五年区间的看空端。
与此同时，VIX虽已从盘中⾼点回落，但仍处于28附近，显⽰市场保护性需求依然旺盛。如果股
市能够守住当前⽔平，隐含波动率可能快速回落，促使此前⼤量买⼊保护性期权的投资者调整
对冲仓位，进⽽形成额外的买盘。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

据⾼盛估计，系统性宏观策略过去⼀周已净卖出约700亿美元全球股票，未来⼀周预计还将卖出
约1000亿美元。不过，⾼盛模型显⽰，进⼀步下跌虽然会压低12⽉初的预期买⼊规模，但不太
可能引发新⼀轮⼤规模、持续性的系统性抛售。
在基准情景下，系统性资⾦有望于12⽉初由卖出转为温和买⼊；如果市场出现⼒度更⼤的反
弹，届时的回补规模甚⾄可能呈现更明显的V形⾛势。
韩国股市遭遇集中减仓，半导体估值降⾄历史低位
韩国股市正承受明显的技术⾯与资⾦⾯压⼒。KOSPI指数周线RSI已降⾄2022年10⽉以来最低
⽔平，同时正在测试100周移动平均线⽀撑，显⽰市场超卖程度显著加深。
对冲基⾦仍在持续削减韩国市场敞⼝，净配置⽐例已降⾄2.3%，处于过去⼀年分布区间的第11
百分位。韩国或将继续成为亚洲市场减仓的主要⽬标。不过，从五年维度看，韩国市场的对冲
基⾦持仓仍相对偏⾼，意味着仓位调整可能尚未结束。
半导体板块的估值压⼒则更加突出。韩国半导体股票⽬前交易于2028年预期市净率约0.7倍，处
于历史低位。⾼盛指出，类似估值⽔平过去曾与半导体周期底部及此后的较好前瞻回报相伴。

不过，低估值并不意味着股价不会继续下跌，当前市场已经计⼊了相当程度的盈利压⼒。
⻩⾦、铜价逼近关键⽀撑，技术性反弹信号浮现
铜价正在逼近重要⽀撑区域，RSI已发出极度超卖信号。感恩节假期与⽉末因素可能暂时缓解持
续的系统性抛售压⼒。尽管⽬前还不能确认铜价已经触底，但技术指标显⽰，市场正在进⼊值
得密切观察的区域。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

写评论
图⽚
⻩⾦同样出现明显反转信号。⾦价盘中触及3400美元这⼀重要⽀撑位后迅速反弹，形成⼤级别
锤形K线。今年3⽉的阶段性低点也曾出现类似形态。
此前，⻩⾦经历了⼤规模投机性抛售，期权波动率及偏斜指标⼀度出现4⾄5个标准差的异常波
动，周线超卖程度则达到2022年以来最严重⽔平。这些信号反映出市场恐慌情绪明显升温，也
使短期反弹的可能性受到关注。
不过，⻩⾦市场的技术⾯损伤仍然严重，美元持续⾛强也构成压制。眼下的反弹能否演变为趋
势反转，仍有待后续价格表现确认。

連接線器 
嘉澤 (3533 TT)

維持評等 
增加持股(Overweight) 
毛利率落底，新平台與新品放量帶動 2027重返成長 
⚫ 3Q26營收創單季新高，但營收動能仍受缺料壓抑 
⚫ 4Q26指引營收低個位數成長，待伺服器新平台爬坡放量 
⚫ QD、SOCAMM與 NPO Socket為第二成長曲線 
3Q26營收創單季新高，但營收動能仍受缺料壓抑：公司於 2026年 11
月 13日舉行法說，3Q26營收 99.58億元(QoQ+4.1%，YoY+18.2%)，
毛利率 44.83%(QoQ-0.64ppts， YoY-6.38ppts)， 營業利益率
22.93%(QoQ+0.96ppts， YoY-7.05ppts)，稅後淨利 19.19 億元
(QoQ-3.28%，YoY-17.2%)，單季 EPS 17.06元。3Q26毛利率雖下滑，
但已收斂，主要受原物料成本、新一代伺服器 CPU平台導入慢於預期
及記憶體缺貨與漲價促使部分桌機客戶回頭採用支援 DDR4、DDR3
的舊平台。3Q26產品組合為伺服器營收占比 53.08%(YoY+55%)，較
前季 55.4%略降，伺服器營收大致呈季持平，反映持續受客戶端零組
件长短料限制整機出貨。成長主要由車用占比 9.19%(YoY+33%)、策
略客戶及桌機小幅回升支撐。桌機占 15.08%(YoY-28%)、筆電占
7.14%(YoY-24%)，PC整體持續萎縮。 
4Q26 指引營收低個位數成長，待伺服器新平台爬坡放量：公司指引
4Q26營收季增低個位數，毛利率受 SOCAMM等新品開始挹注，預期
小幅回升。2027年展望伺服器營收增速有望高於 2026年，明年預估
伺服器出貨量年增 10-15%，多數客戶預估成長達 30-50%，但受限於
零組件供應，公司較為保守，若供給改善則有上修空間。伺服器平台
進展方面，AMD Venice現滲透率低於 3%，年底將達 3-5%，預期自
1Q27季底加速放量，估計 2027年平均滲透率約 20%。Intel現 Eagle 
Stream與 Birch Stream出貨比重約各半，預期 1H27 Birch Stream逾
60%，Oak Stream延後至 1Q27季底或 2Q27季初起量，若如期推進，
2H27採用率估 10~15%，下半年貢獻將高於上半年。此外，微軟 Cobalt 
CPU Socket將於 2H27開始貢獻。 
QD、SOCAMM與 NPO Socket為第二成長曲線：QD接頭現產能滿
載，已規劃未來幾季產能翻倍，7M26營收占比由 1%提升至 8M262%，
12M26 將達 3-5%，展望 2027年營收占比將近 10%，成長顯著。而
SOCAMM已開始貢獻營收，8M26營收占比約 2%，預期後續將顯著
成長。NPO Socket預期 2027年小量貢獻，營收占比約低於 2%，預估
顯著放量將於 2028年。 
我們預估 2026、2027年 EPS分別為 75.9、108.37元，基於 2027預估 
EPS x 20倍本益比，目標價為 2,170元，維持增加持股之投資評等建
議。風險因子：新品 CPU平台導入進度遞延、原物料成本上漲風險。

## Document 635: 宏观经济深度报告：雄关漫道真如铁—2027年国内宏观主线

Source PDF: all report pdf/宏观经济深度报告：雄关漫道真如铁—2027年国内宏观主线.pdf
Report source: 国信证券
Date: 2026-11-26
Pages: 21
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

S0980524090003 S0980513100001
基础数据
固定资产投资累计同比 -8.10
社零总额当月同比 0.40
出口当月同比 35.20
M2 7.14
市场走势
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理
相关研究报告
《美丽新世界——“科技-政治”双周期下的若干思考》 ——
2026-11-22
《宏观经济深度报告-中期选举后的“变”与“不变”》 ——
2026-11-03
《宏观经济深度报告-有形之手（7）：年内财政“托”得住经济
吗？》 ——2026-09-10
《黄油与大炮——追赶中的 K 型与哑铃策略》 ——2026-08-21
《宏观经济深度报告-黄油与大炮——新政策框架下的资产逻
辑》 ——2026-08-14
回头看，“不按剧本走”才是2026真正的主线。展望2027，“科技+政治”
双周期格局延续，但中美“攻守易位”，关键在于拎清明年的工作重心。
核心诉求：求稳＞谋远＞守底
2027年经济工作不宜按常规年份来理解。明年是“十五五”规划集中落实的
关键之年，也是党的二十一大召开之年。因此，做好明年经济工作，要从党

和国家事业的全局出发进行把握。我们将其归纳为“一个中心、两个大局”：
以迎接重要会议为中心，统筹科技优先与合理增长两个大局。
明年增长目标或效仿近几次会议年份，小幅调整为“4.5%左右”。但在多元
约束下，大概率选择“通胀换增长”，重回供强需弱加剧、PPI承压的格局。
现实挑战：总需求面临“三座大山”
①外需拉动减弱，算力出口承压。AI资本开支进入“戒断期”，海外高利率
压制需求，贸易壁垒也将进一步升级，硅基出口面临量、价和基数三重压力。
②化债与地产拖累投资。“退平台”与“清欠”双节点，挤占地方财力与基
建开支；“828”新政下房企拿地、开工谨慎，压制地产投资。
③收入预期制约消费修复。收入“K型”分化、财富缩水和“国补”透支，
共同约束居民支出。
破局关键：因势利导、守正出奇
明年“求稳”框定上限，改革与政策难有实质突破；“谋远”指向科技仍是
最大确定性；“守底”则意味着增速底线不容有失。明年并非简单“保数据”，
结构上仍有较多抓手有望带来预期差。
①发挥制度优势，期待中美AI基建“温差”。我国算力规模总体落后，结

构上“美国缺电、中国缺芯”。海外AI投资步幅放缓、算力产业链产能分
配格局松动，正是我国加大跨周期投入的战略机遇，坚定不移加快算力基础
设施建设和核心科技攻关，形成“自研为主＋多元抢购”的双重模式。
②用好政策空间，腾挪修补、结构优化。稳投资以中央加力和结构性扩张为
主，中央预算内投资和“两重”加力、“六张网”承接，高技术、AI算力与
垂类应用成为制造业投资主增量，预计固投回稳至0%。促消费从撬动转向赋
能，个税优化和育儿补贴加力扩围是潜在方向，预计社零回升至2.0%左右。
稳出口依靠结构优化与汇率弹性，传统制造业、新兴市场和电网设备等有望
接力，稳定外贸基本盘，预计出口回落至9%附近。
③统筹货币财政，维持总量强度、重在结构调整。财政要“扛大旗”，不依
赖赤字率突破，而通过中央加杠杆、专项债扩容和政策性金融工具加码腾挪
挖潜。货币政策考验操作艺术，维持“适度宽松”但“推绳子”难度加大。
双降空间保留，由风险突发事件激活；LPR调整优先级更高，推动“换锚”
向贷款传导；此外，“结构降息＋专项贴息”有望成为精准降成本标配。

风险提示：海外波动加剧，政策调整滞后，经济增速下滑。

### Page 5

回头看，“不按剧本走”也许才是 2026 真正的宏观主线。放眼全球，在“科技+
政治”双周期叠加下，宏观波动高度非线性，传统假设沦为反指，“编外”事件
成为“基准情形”。落到国内，“十五五”开局经济运行大体平稳，输入性因素
反而占据主导，算力繁荣、中选前地缘博弈、美联储掉头成为关键变量。
展望 2027，“双周期”格局延续，但中美“攻守易位”主动权将回归国内。如何
从纷杂的头绪中拎出主要矛盾，关键是弄清明年的主线任务，及其优先次序。我
们不妨从这开始。
1.核心诉求：一个中心、两个大局
一个基本判断是，2027年经济工作不宜作为常规年度来理解。明年是“十五五”
规划集中落实的关键之年，也是党的二十一大召开之年。因此，做好明年经济工
作，要从党和国家事业的全局出发进行把握。我们将其归纳为“一个中心、两个
大局”：前者是总前提，后者是总前提下的两方面具体工作。
一个中心是迎接重要会议胜利召开，本质是“求稳”。
从内部看，党的二十一大是“在基本实现社会主义现代化的关键时期、向第二个
百年奋斗目标进军新征程上召开的一次十分重要的代表大会”，承担着总结过去

五年、明确未来五年前进方向和行动纲领的功能。从外部看，随着美国中选“蓝
潮”横扫带来的“行政-立法”对立以及共和党内分裂，美国进入“后特朗普时代”，
我国严峻的外部环境总体缓和，客观上可以将注意力更多转向国内。
明年工作的基本方针也因此明确：稳字当头、统筹兼顾。各级政府将转入“风险
厌恶”模式，对经济平稳运行、社会大局稳定的诉求显著上升。一方面，宏观政
策难以明显收缩，重大项目与民生保障将保持必要强度。另一方面，一些重大改
革举措将延后落地。一言蔽之，“多言数穷，不如守中”。
图1：重要会议年中央对“稳定”关注度提高 图2：同期地方对“稳定”关注度同样提高
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理
在这一基本前提下，“科技优先”与“合理增长”两个大局要坚定不移、统筹兼
顾。本质上，这是“谋远”与“守底”的关系。
当前，社会上对两者存在一些争议。今年以来，在 AI 浪潮的席卷下，经济 K 型分
化，新锐“硅基”是否对主流“碳基”形成挤出？进一步说，科技与民生孰轻孰

重？我们认为，不应将两者简单对立，而是放在我国发展的优先次序中辩证看待。
首先要明确，“科技优先”是当前的基本国策。今年“730”政治局会议以来，“加

### Page 6

快推动新旧动能转换”被多次强调，意味着中央判断当前经济整体承压是动能转
换期的客观产物，改善经济的根本在于压缩这一过程，因而需要加码“超常规措
施”，帮助新技术和新动能尽快“决定性突破”。
“科技优先”并非一时之策，而是我国推动动能接续的必然选择。回顾这一进程，
“十四五”开局大力度整改地产，传统“碳基”发展模式走向尾声。5年后“828”
新规落地，事实上标志着地产过渡期已基本结束，钢筋混凝土不再作为拉动增长
的主引擎。未来“碳基”逆周期政策将进一步边缘化。与此同时，做好动能接续
成为“十五五”的关键命题，恰逢 AI产业变革，其背后的“硅基”链条，从能源、
芯片、模型再到更上游的矿产、人才、生态，成为决策者关注的重点。
图3：近年来我国 GDP与增长趋势出现偏离 图4：同期房地产增加值占比跌落
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理
这一关键节点，又恰逢外部政治环境发生重大变化，“科技优先”的紧迫性进一
步加强。美国民主党“横扫”后，叠加关键企业格局调整，少数游说者和理性声

音式微，对华科技管制料将进一步收紧，我国追赶窗口期收窄。
与之对应，转换期经济也要兜住“合理增长”的底线。合理的本质是遵循经济运
行的内生规律，不刻意追求高增速。在动能转换期，若一味“恋战”通过“碳基”
刺激对冲磨底压力，不仅可能耗费大量资源，更可能延缓转型、贻误战机。因此，
2025年中央经济工作会议首提的“超常规”一词，已从经济政策改为科技攻关，
这反映了中央对于发展思路的重要转变。对经济表现的定性，也自此从总量性的
“稳中有进”改为结构性的“向新向优”。
图5：从生产端观察，2026年 K型分化显著加剧 图6：完成2035目标所要求的未来增速测算
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理

### Page 7

但是，这不意味着明年增长目标可以“撒手不管”。主要出于三方面考虑：①增
速即民生，转型初期的陡峭斜率下，经济裂口拉大，绝大多数的普通劳动者体感
将显著弱于总体 GDP 增速，“下半撇”将成为关注重心；②引导预期，中高速增
长关键点位（4.5%以上）一旦失守，未来稳增长、稳市场难度更大；③长期要求，
实现 2035 远景目标要求“十五五”“十六五”实际复合增长 4.2%，增速过早下
滑将使后续压力陡增。
做个小结，“求稳 > 谋远 > 守底”，是明年我国宏观目标的优先级。明年增长
目标或将效仿近几个会议年份的做法，小幅调整为“4.5%左右”。但在多元约束
下，大概率选择通过“通胀换增长”，重回供强需弱加剧、PPI承压的宏观格局。
图7：近三次重要会议所在年份经济增长目标下调
资料来源：Wind、政府工作报告、国信证券经济研究所整理
2.现实挑战：需求需求需求
我们面临的现实是，明年总需求可能出现“三座大山”。
图8：需求可能成为明年主要压力源 图9：全球主要央行纷纷加息，联储利率中枢偏高
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理

一是出口带动效应减弱。主要是三重压力：
①AI资本开支进入“戒断期”。美国 AI 基建正面临电力与并网瓶颈，同时，“AI

### Page 8

民粹主义”（AI populism）下两党围绕电价、监管等“伟光正”议题反复拉扯，
将抬升项目成本，导致 AI 资本开支“二阶导”拐折。今年我国出口高增有赖硅基
产品“量价齐升”，明年则可能面临数量、价格与基数的“三杀”。
②海外政策环境收紧，高利率压制需求。能源抬升全球通胀中枢，货币政策短期
难现宽松。预计美联储 12 月再加息一次，政策利率升至 4%以上；往后即便中东
局势缓和，明年高利率中枢仍会压制居民消费、企业补库及资本开支。
③面临多维贸易壁垒。除中美外，中欧产业竞争与贸易失衡问题交织，欧盟态度
强硬，正通过反补贴调查、价格承诺及外国补贴条例（FSR）等工具加强对华贸易
壁垒。仅就我国占据优势的“新三样”看，混动及插混出口面临“四年减半”限
制；光伏、电池等优势产业面临类似压力，未来环境料将进一步恶化。
图10：海外 AI云厂商资本开支预计放缓 图11：出口高增背后，能源与硅基带来的涨价效应不容忽视
资料来源：Wind、上市公司官网、美国证券交易所、国信证券经济研
究所绘制
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所绘制。气泡大小代表我国1-10

月累计出口规模
二是“630”与“828”压制投资。曾几何时，投资是稳经济的压舱石。而当下情
况似乎出现反转：
①化债清欠收官，压制基建投资。“双 630”成为明年的关键节点，城投“退平
台”和“清欠”两大任务在年中集中收官，上半年地方财力将被显著挤占，基建
相应承压；此后，资金腾挪压力有望阶段性缓解。
从“退平台”进度看，今年 9 月全国剩余平台数较 2023年 3月下降 92%，6 月末
经营性债务减少 79%。下降较慢主要是平台退出要求隐债清零：前期大量小平台
退出后，经营性债务转为普通国企债务，对规模影响不大；但剩余平台体量大、
清零难，退出前负债仍纳入经营性口径，无法“压缩”。而这些平台多是区县级
或重点地区尾部城市，可盘活资产少、财政回旋余地小，成为“硬骨头”。此外，
退平台后经营性债务接续、基层“三保”仍在分流现金，对新增隐债和融资也将
进一步趋严，基建难有强反弹。
②“828”新政进一步传导，地产投资探底。新政事实上将期房转为现房模式，开
发商回款与融资延后，将进一步拖累新开工和施工等环节。此外，房企拿地更趋

谨慎，土地出让收入可能从今年约 3万亿继续大幅下行至 2万亿左右。

### Page 9

图12：明年上半年隐债化解仍有压力 图13：土地相关的财政收入继续承压
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理
三是收入预期继续压制消费，叠加耐用品消费被“国补”前置：
①钱没有很好流入“需要的人”手中。GDP、居民人均可支配收入和消费支出实际
增速梯次回落，反映经济增长对居民消费的传导受阻。这一情况明年可能延续：
“新经济”目前仍是少数人的游戏和多数人的代价。收入处于中位的“主流”群
体改善较慢，实际体感弱于平均水平。若明年 K 型分化延续，新增收入将持续向
消费倾向较低的少数高收入群体集中，对消费不利。
图14：消费支出弱于经济和收入增速 图15：“主流”居民的收入改善相对较慢
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理
②居民财产持续缩水。以住房为例，其仍占居民家庭财富近五成。一线城市房价
较前期高点回落逾 35%，且尚未明显企稳；租金等收入减少、房价预期偏弱，也
会推高预防性储蓄、促使居民提前还贷，进一步挤压消费。

③“国补”效果趋于饱和。截至今年 10月，限上“国补类”商品零售额累计同比
下降 8.9%，明显弱于核心消费类的 2.6%，开始“反噬”社零。考虑到耐用品更新
周期较长，短期内新增需求难以接续，低基数难抵消透支效应。若明年补贴继续
退坡，政策托底作用将持续弱化。

3.破局关键：因势利导、守正出奇
挑战之中蕴含转机，破局在于因势利导。明年“求稳”划定上限，改革与政策难
有实质突破；“谋远”指向科技仍是最大确定性；“守底”则意味着增速底线不
容有失。

综合来看，我们认为明年仍可实现不低于 4.5%的经济增速。
①顶层设计：“守底”要求经济不能失速，预计 2027年实际 GDP目标设定“4.5%
左右”，实际完成不低于 4.5%。
②供需错位：为保障这一增长目标，即便总需求可能偏弱，但工业生产将维持刚
性，预计增速在 5.0%附近。
③价格承压：在供需错位的情况下，叠加外部涨价因素消退，预计 PPI可能“打
回原型”，下半年显著转负；生猪价格和农产品对 CPI形成支撑，但油价回落和
消费偏弱限制其上行，预计 CPI上涨 1.0%左右。对应名义 GDP增速 5.0%。

图18：今年 PPI的三项主要贡献明年均可能回落 图19：明年 CPI或维持温和水平
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理
尽管如此，明年并非简单“保数据”，结构上仍有较多作用点能带来预期差。
一是发挥制度优势：中美“龟兔赛跑”，算力投资大有可为。
算力是 AI时代的原油，中美存在显著差距，算力投资大有可为。2026年美国 AI
算力规模约为中国的 6.4倍
1
，结构上美国缺电、中国缺芯，各方均在补短板。
美国先发优势明显，但近期步幅放缓；我国既然看准 AI 作为新一轮技术革命主赛
道的战略意义，并把突破包括芯片在内的核心科技作为既定国策，就不会跟随美
国节奏。此前“算力繁荣”下的涨价潮有所缓和，全球算力产业链的产能分配格
局出现松动，正是我国加大跨周期投入力度的机遇期，自研为主+多渠道补足。

二是用好政策空间：腾挪修补，结构优化。
换届年总量强度难有实质突破，但结构上仍有腾挪空间。
①稳投资，中央加力和结构性扩张为主。2027年必须重新激活投资，推动基建由
负转正，并稳住制造业投资。预计明年地产投资继续深跌，但固投回稳至 0%左右。

一方面，中央接力基建，“六张网”形成项目抓手。预计中央预算内投资将继续
扩大至 7900 亿元；“两重”建设资金有望增至 1 万亿元；“六张网”规模合计约
25 万亿元，将成为主要载体。叠加低基数，预计带动明年基建投资回升至 4.0%。

另一方面，制造业与新基建联动，高技术、AI 算力与垂类应用等方向成为主增量。

### Page 13

今年企业盈利修复，为资本开支扩张打下基础：1-9 月，工业企业利润同比增长
15.5%，其中制造业增长 17.0%；同时，高技术产业投资增长 4.9%，其中电子电路
制造业增长 58.7%，信息服务业增长 20.2%。后续叠加财政补贴、再贷款等支持力
度加大，明年制造业投资有望回升至 2.0%左右。
②促消费，从撬动转向赋能。“国补”刺激效应递减，应放弃“花小钱办大事”
的思路，回归增收的本源。中央多次强调要切实增加居民获得感，其中个税调整
和育儿补贴均在选项中，群众基础好、见效快。在政策支持的基础上，叠加汽车
等耐用品出现低基数，预计社零回升至 2.0%附近。
调高个税起征点。现行基本减除标准为每月 5000 元，而 2025年全国居民人均可
支配收入已达每月 3615元。根据近 4 次上调起征点的规律看，目前窗口已经打开。
此外，1-10 月个税收入同比增加 22.9%，高收入群体成为主要贡献，客观上也为
调节税收结构打下基础。
提高育儿补贴力度。现行标准为每孩每年 3600 元，2025 年落地以来社会反馈积

极。2027年受益补贴的三年滚动人口预计下降约 420万人，维持单孩标准不变将
使补贴总额自然缩减约 15.7%。因此，有必要适时加力扩围。
图28：我国当前个税起征点已维持约 8年 图29：生育补贴总额随出生人口下降而收缩
资料来源：国家统计局、Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：国家统计局、Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理
③稳出口，结构优化与汇率弹性稳定出口基本盘。算力出口是典型的“叫好不叫
座”，进出口同升同降，对顺差影响有限。同时，我国“硅基”“碳基”出口有
望接力对冲，叠加人民币单边升值预期缓解，明年传统商品出口有望维稳。我们
判断明年出口增速或回落至 9%左右。
一方面，顺差对出口韧性“脱敏”。一定要区分出口和顺差对于经济的影响。今
年我国 AI出口出现“来料加工”特征，体现为算力品出口的扩张，伴随着芯片、
存储等高附加值中间品进口高增。因此顺差并未显著强于季节性，对 GDP增长的
贡献更显著低于传统出口主导的 2025年。随着海外算力开支放缓，算力品出口承
压的同时，进口也会同步回落，形成对冲。

另一方面，结构轮动与人民币升值趋缓，有望稳定外贸基本盘。美国 AI资本开支
放缓，但其“缺电”逻辑仍将支撑电网、变压器等设备需求，形成产业链内部接
力。同时，算力相关电子产品占我国出口不足 20%，占 80%以上的传统制造业和新
兴市场仍是出口“基本盘”。
此外，11 月 3 日，央行上调外汇存款准备金率 2个百分点，释放出防范人民币过
快升值、稳定汇率预期的政策信号。明年随着出口增速回落、结汇需求减弱，叠
加强美元扰动，人民币升值动能进一步趋缓，为传统商品出口提供适宜环境。

三是统筹货币财政：维持总量强度，重在结构调整。
财政是重心，在总需求不足和地方被束缚的情况下，中央财政要“扛大旗”。主
要举措是维持广义赤字率、加码中央支出和准财政工具，以对冲地方收缩压力。
预计 2027年财政力度自然扩张，即狭义赤字率（4%）、广义赤字率（8.1%）基本

不变。不依赖赤字率突破，而是通过中央加杠杆、专项债扩容和政策性金融工具
加码等手段腾挪挖潜，聚焦预算的实际落地能力，提升一二本账支出完成度。

### Page 15

图34：政府广义赤字规模扩张 图35：我国政府杠杆率正在快速上行
资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所整理 资料来源：Wind、国信证券经济研究所绘制
①总量温和扩张，增量更多来自广义财政工具。
中央历来对提升预算赤字率十分谨慎，2025年在预判外部挑战加剧的情况下，决
定加大“超常规”逆周期调节，方突破 3%这一心理关口。明年保持 4%的赤字率，
既合理兜底内需，也为未来发力留有空间。假设 2027 年名义 GDP 增长 5%，狭义
赤字规模预计约 6.23 万亿元，较今年增加约 3400亿元。
第二本帐上，预计新增专项债加码到 4.8 万亿元，对冲置换债发行结束后的地方
财力缺口；超长期特别国债增至 1.5 万亿元，其中“两重”1 万亿元、设备更新
2000亿元、消费“国补”预计 2000-2500 亿元并可能拓展至服务消费、财政金融
协同专项 1000亿元，增量基本填补注资特别国债停发后的空档。上述工具与财政
赤字合计形成约 12.53万亿元广义赤字，较今年增加 6400 亿元。

此外，四季度大概率仍按惯例，使用地方债结存限额约 5000亿元，补充地方综合
财力；政策性金融工具增至 1 万亿元，主要考虑其不增加显性债务、资金撬动能
力较强，并可满足“六张网”等重大项目的资本金需求。
